## 124. MAWAM: Joint Imagination for Collaborative Robot Manipulation with a Multi-Agent World Action Model

**中文标题**: MAWAM: Joint Imagination for Collaborative Robot Manipulation with a Multi-Agent World Action Model

**作者**: Rongze Tang, Chenyu Zhou, Jianjie Fang, Ziming Liu, Zhaolu Wang et al.

**发布日期**: 2026-11-22

**arXiv ID**: [2611.29501v1](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29501v1)

**PDF**: [下载链接](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29501v1)

**类别**: cs.RO, cs.AI

<details>
<summary><b>摘要 (Abstract)</b></summary>

World action models (WAMs) excel at robot manipulation by imagining future observations and decoding actions from them, yet remain confined to a single embodied agent. In practice, manipulation often requires multiple robots to collaborate across disjoint workspaces, coupled timing, and heterogeneous embodiments. Such coordination demands both spatiotemporal alignment of future interactions and awareness of individual embodiment and role identities, whereas existing decentralized per-robot policies, flat centralized models, and multi-robot VLAs fail to unite both. To bridge this gap, we present MAWAM, a multi-agent world action model that jointly predicts the future observations of multiple embodiments and decodes each agent's actions from them for collaborative manipulation. Through an agent-axis curriculum, MAWAM expands a single-agent WAM along an agent dimension to coordinate teams under shared weights. Within one unified causal sequence, joint self-attention spans all agents to spatiotemporally align their imagined futures, while hierarchical language, identity encodings, and embodiment masks bind each stream to its role, identity, and embodiment. We further formalize multi-agent-necessary manipulation along three axes (why, when, and who) and develop MA-Task Harness, a coding-agent pipeline where mutually constraining generators and evaluators co-evolve customizable simulation-data and evaluation packages. Using this pipeline, we build MA-Bench across all axes with seven tasks, pairing joint success rate with role and collaboration scores for fine-grained analysis. On MA-Bench, MAWAM reaches 82.7% mean joint success, 30.7% above the strongest baseline, and achieves real-time closed-loop collaboration on a real heterogeneous robot team.

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>中文摘要</b></summary>

World action models (WAMs) excel at robot manipulation by imagining future observations and decoding actions from them, yet remain confined to a single embodied agent. In practice, manipulation often requires multiple robots to collaborate across disjoint workspaces, coupled timing, and heterogeneous embodiments. Such coordination demands both spatiotemporal alignment of future interactions and awareness of individual embodiment and role identities, whereas existing decentralized per-robot policies, flat centralized models, and multi-robot VLAs fail to unite both. To bridge this gap, we present MAWAM, a multi-agent world action model that jointly predicts the future observations of multiple embodiments and decodes each agent's actions from them for collaborative manipulation. Through an agent-axis curriculum, MAWAM expands a single-agent WAM along an agent dimension to coordinate teams under shared weights. Within one unified causal sequence, joint self-attention spans all agents to spatiotemporally align their imagined futures, while hierarchical language, identity encodings, and embodiment masks bind each stream to its role, identity, and embodiment. We further formalize multi-agent-necessary manipulation along three axes (why, when, and who) and develop MA-Task Harness, a coding-agent pipeline where mutually constraining generators and evaluators co-evolve customizable simulation-data and evaluation packages. Using this pipeline, we build MA-Bench across all axes with seven tasks, pairing joint success rate with role and collaboration scores for fine-grained analysis. On MA-Bench, MAWAM reaches 82.7% mean joint success, 30.7% above the strongest baseline, and achieves real-time closed-loop collaboration on a real heterogeneous robot team.

</details>

## 125. Self-Refining VLA Sampling

**中文标题**: Self-Refining VLA Sampling

**作者**: Jeff Duan, Arjun Sharma, Jennifer Cruden, Vansh Tibrewal

**发布日期**: 2026-11-22

**arXiv ID**: [2611.29383v1](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29383v1)

**PDF**: [下载链接](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.29383v1)

**类别**: cs.RO

<details>
<summary><b>摘要 (Abstract)</b></summary>

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models typically use flow-based generative modeling to learn a rich, often multimodal, distribution of robot actions. Generating samples from flow-based methods involves iteratively updating random noise, and distinct noise samples from the same state can result in distinct trajectories and task outcomes. Existing test-time scaling methods improve action chunk generation via selection, aggregation, or correction, but commonly require the generation of large ensembles of candidates or the training of an external corrector. We adapt Predict-and-Perturb (P&P), a self-refinement procedure for flow-based models originally used for mode-seeking in video generation, to the robotics domain. We find that applying P&P to VLA models requires modifications to the refinement process used in video generation due to differences in the underlying structure of the data. Across 400 episodes of LIBERO evaluated using SmolVLA, P&P improves upon the stock baseline by 5.3 percentage points. Further, episode-level P\&P uncertainty correlates strongly with task failure (AUC 0.851).

</details>

<details>
<summary><b>中文摘要</b></summary>

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models typically use flow-based generative modeling to learn a rich, often multimodal, distribution of robot actions. Generating samples from flow-based methods involves iteratively updating random noise, and distinct noise samples from the same state can result in distinct trajectories and task outcomes. Existing test-time scaling methods improve action chunk generation via selection, aggregation, or correction, but commonly require the generation of large ensembles of candidates or the training of an external corrector. We adapt Predict-and-Perturb (P&P), a self-refinement procedure for flow-based models originally used for mode-seeking in video generation, to the robotics domain. We find that applying P&P to VLA models requires modifications to the refinement process used in video generation due to differences in the underlying structure of the data. Across 400 episodes of LIBERO evaluated using SmolVLA, P&P improves upon the stock baseline by 5.3 percentage points. Further, episode-level P\&P uncertainty correlates strongly with task failure (AUC 0.851).

</details>
